Selectivity and matrix effect	6 lots of matrix blanks: The response at the retention time of the analyte in 5 out of 6 matrix blanks must be ≤20% of the mean analyte peak response of the LLOQ. The response at the retention time of the IS in 5 out of 6 matrix blanks must be ≤5% of the mean peak response of the IS of the LLOQ. 6 lots of matrix blanks with IS: The response at the retention time of the analyte in 5 out of 6 matrix blanks with IS must be ≤20% of the mean analyte response of the LLOQ. Pooled matrix blanks with IS: If there is a peak area response at the analyte retention time, the peak area ratio must be ≤20% of the peak area ratio of the LLOQ. ULOQ pooled matrix with analyte only: The response at the retention time of the IS must be ≤5% of the mean IS peak area of the LLOQ.
There was no interference observed at the retention time of the analyte.
Interference and specificity	Analytical performance of AT2221 low quality control (1.50 ng/mL) was tested in human plasma spiked with ATB200 (1500 µg/mL). Overall %RE was -12.67%. No interference was observed at the retention time of the analyte with coadministered drug ATB200.
Hemolysis effect	Two concentrations of QCs (QCL: 1.50 ng/mL and QCH: 200 ng/mL) were tested at 2% hemolysis. The cumulative %RE was 0.67% (for QCL), and -4.00% (for QCH) of nominal in the samples tested, which met the acceptance criteria (%RE within ±15% of nominal).
Lipemic effect	Hyperlipidemia was tested in 2 concentrations of QCs (QCL: 1.50 ng/mL; QCH: 200 ng/mL). The cumulative %RE was 5.33% (for QCL) and -2.00% (for QCH) of nominal in the samples tested, which met the acceptance criteria (%RE within ±15% of nominal).

L2008: ## Patients with Missing FVC Values at Week 52
L2009: A total of 16 patients (13% in each treatment group) were missing the FVC measure at Week 52. Figure 94 presents the FVC observations for these 16 patients. Solid lines in the plot indicate patients who discontinued the trial and dashed lines indicate those who did not discontinue. When the LOCF approach is used to impute missing values, only the postbaseline values are used to impute, meaning that Subject (b) (6) is excluded from analysis of FVC.

L2047: ## **Sensitivity Analysis of FVC Observations with Unacceptable Grade**
L2048: An information request (IR023) was sent to the Applicant on January 4, 2022 requesting clarification on inclusion of 13 FVC observations from forced spirometries that had an unacceptable grade assessed by [REDACTED](b)(4). The Applicant explained that these data were included in the derived analysis datasets because they were graded as acceptable or borderline acceptable by a second consulting company. The review team performed a sensitivity analysis by excluding these 13 FVC observations. This impacted 6 subjects in total: 2 subjects [REDACTED](b)(6) were excluded due to both screening measures having an unacceptable [REDACTED](b)(4) grade, 4 subjects [REDACTED](b)(6) had adjusted baseline values due to one screening measurement having an unacceptable [REDACTED](b)(4) grade, and 2 of those subjects had measures omitted at Week 12 (Subject [REDACTED](b)(6)), Week 26 (Subject [REDACTED](b)(6)), and Week 52 (Subject [REDACTED](b)(6)) due to an unacceptable [REDACTED](b)(4) grade.
L2049: **Table 152** presents the results of this sensitivity analysis. The estimated treatment difference was 2.38 (95% CI: 0.04, 4.71; p=0.0460), slightly larger than 2.32 (95% CI: 0.02, 4.62; p=0.0484) obtained based on the datasets that included these 13 observations.

L2056: ## **Sensitivity Analysis of 6MWT with Deviations**
L2057: **Table 153** presents the 15 patients who had at least one 6MWT with a deviation at the screening or week 52/EOS timepoints. The sensitivity analysis was limited to ERT-experienced patients, and 13 of the 15 affected patients were ERT-experienced. Patients who had all their week 52 observations impacted were removed from the sensitivity analysis. Patients who did not have observations at week 52 and their EOS observation had a deviation were removed from the sensitivity analysis. For patients who had only one of two screening observations impacted or only one of two week 52 observations impacted, their observation without a deviation was used instead of the average of the two measures. This resulted in 8 patients being removed from the sensitivity analysis, 2 patients with adjusted screening values, and 3 patients with adjusted week 52 values.

L2089: ## 19. Other Drug Development Considerations: Additional Information and Assessment
L2090: As part of its quality module, Amicus submitted a comparative analytical assessment to support a scientific bridge between the non-U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product used in trial ATB200-03 and a U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product (Lumizyme). The Agency reviewed this submission and found that Amicus did not provide adequate data or information to establish an acceptable scientific bridge, including because the assessment did not include analysis of some highly critical quality attributes. In addition, the review team consulted with CDER's Office of Regulatory Policy because Amicus's assessment included various types of information that the Agency would not rely upon in determining whether an adequate scientific bridge was established. The Agency believed that the inadequacy of Amicus's comparative analytical assessment could be addressed by the addition of a labeling statement rather than the submission of additional analytical data and, to that end, recommended the following statement (which reflects regulatory policy input) in cipa-mig labeling to clarify that the clinical trial data from ATB200-03 is adequate to support the safety, purity, and potency, i.e., effectiveness of cipa-mig for use as a second-line therapy according to recommendations in the approved labeling but that no conclusions can be drawn from the clinical trial regarding comparative effectiveness:
L2091: *A U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product was not used in this clinical trial.
Conclusions cannot be drawn from this clinical trial regarding comparative effectiveness
between a U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product and POMBILITI in combination
with Opfolda for the treatment of adult patients with LOPD weighing \ge 40 kg and who
are not improving on their current ERT.*
L2092: In the absence of an adequate scientific bridge, Amicus is unable to make comparative claims of effectiveness between a U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product and cipa-mig. In addition, aspects of the design and results of trial ATB200-03 create additional complexities related to comparative claims even if Amicus established an acceptable scientific bridge between the non-U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product used in the trial and U.S.-licensed Lumizyme.
L2093: ## 20. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of Scientific Investigations, Other Inspections)
L2094: ATB200-03 was the single pivotal trial, with no single site enrolling more than 11 subjects. Selection of clinical sites for inspection was based on the number of subjects enrolled, outliers for efficacy endpoints, inspection history, and geographic location. Site 2063 (United States) was inspected, and sites 4002 (Australia) and 4005 (Australia) participated in a Remote Regulatory Assessment (RRA). Due to travel restrictions from the COVID-19 pandemic, the inspection planned for site 9004 (Denmark) could not be conducted. The inspection and RRAs verified results of the 6MWT, the primary efficacy endpoint, and FVC (% predicted), the key secondary efficacy endpoint.
L2095: Inspection of site 4005 was planned, in part, to obtain more information for subject [REDACTED](b) (6) who was randomized to a non-U.S.-approved alglucosidase alfa product coadministered with placebo and who the Applicant identified as a statistical outlier for the 6MWT and sitting FVC (see Section 6.2.1.3.1). Based on information obtained during the RRA, there was insufficient evidence to support exclusion of this subject from the primary analysis population.
L2096: The review team identified 27 patients with a protocol deviation for the 6MWT described by the Applicant as “6MWT not done or not completed correctly as described in the functional assessment manual.” For 15 of those patients, this deviation occurred in either both or only one of the two assessments performed at screening or Week 52, the timepoints used for the efficacy analyses; the Applicant included this data in the analysis for efficacy. In the Applicant’s response to an information request (received on January 13, 2022) regarding error codes in the spirometry reports, the Applicant clarified that 13 sitting FVC (% predicted) spirometry values for 6 patients received a Quality Control (QC) grade of unacceptable. The QC grade for the spirometry was assigned centrally, before the database lock and unblinding, by a consultant. For those 6 patients, the unacceptable QC grade was for 8 spirometry tests performed at screening and one spirometry test at Week 52. The data from these spirometry tests were included in the data analyses after a reassessment based in the 2005 ATS/ERS Standardization of Spirometry guidelines (Graham et al. 2019). The review team performed sensitivity analyses to assess the robustness of the results in relation to the data from patients with 6MWT protocol deviations and FVC (% predicted) QC grades rated as unacceptable (see Section 16).
L2097: The review team concluded that inspection of one site in the United States and the RRA at the two sites in Australia was sufficient to assess the data quality and integrity. The Office of Scientific Investigations concluded that, other than the issues described above, the clinical data collected at the three sites appeared reliable. No significant trial conduct issues or regulatory violations were identified at any of the three sites. Overall, the improvement in motor and pulmonary function is minimally affected by the identified discrepancies and would not change our regulatory decision.
L2098: See the Clinical Inspection Summary dated April 5, 2021 for additional details.

## Pombiliti (cipaglucosidase alfa-atga) and Opfolda (miglustat)
L2134: Postmarketing Commitments	Milestones
4. To perform extractables/leachables studies and risk assessments to evaluate leachables from the container closure system(s) and manufacturing product conduct surfaces of cipaglucosidase alfa drug substance and drug product and assess the potential impact of leachables on product quality at the end of drug product shelf-life. The analyses will be performed using drug substance and drug product lot(s) and/or representative samples (e.g., [REDACTED](b) (4) if justified) analyzed at appropriate time points, including at the end of drug product shelf life. Appropriate methods will be used to detect, identify, and quantify organic nonvolatile, volatile and semivolatile species, and metals. Characterization of the potential impact of leachables on product quality will be assessed using adequate analytical methods. Complete data and the risk evaluation for the potential impact of leachables on product safety and quality will be provided in the final study report per 21 CFR 601.12.	Final report submission:
March, 2023
5. To develop, validate, and implement a test method with justified numerical acceptance criteria for control of cipaglucosidase alfa kinetic parameters during drug product release and stability testing. Critical kinetic parameters (e.g., Km, Kcat, etc.,) will be determined, justified, and included in the control strategy. If applicable, justification and data supporting [REDACTED](b) (4) will be provided. The method validation report for the enzyme kinetic assay, the revised drug product release and stability specifications, and all supporting studies and data will be provided in a final study report per 21 CFR 601.12.	Final report submission:
March, 2024

L2229: Figure 1 displays three box plots comparing miglustat exposure metrics between two body weight groups: >=50 kg (n=76) and 40 to <50 kg (n=3). The left plot shows Miglustat Conc 1 hr Post-dose (ng/mL), the middle plot shows Miglustat Conc 5 hr Post-dose (ng/mL), and the right plot shows AUCss of Miglustat (ng.h/mL). In all three plots, the distributions for both body weight groups are highly overlapping, indicating similar exposure levels. The 40 to <50 kg group has a significantly smaller sample size (n=3) compared to the >=50 kg group (n=76).

L2232: Figure 4 displays four scatter plots showing the Maximum Change From Baseline of Urine Glucose Tetrasaccharide (HEX4) and Plasma Creatinine Kinase (CK) versus exposure metrics (AUC[0-24h] or C_{max}) for two treatments: ATB200/AT2221 (red dots) and Alglucosidase Alfa/Placebo (blue dots). The plots are arranged in a 2x2 grid.
The top row plots show Urine Glucose Tetrasaccharide (HEX4) change from baseline (mmol/moCr) on the Y-axis, ranging from -20 to 20. The bottom row plots show Plasma Creatinine Kinase (CK) change from baseline (IU/L) on the Y-axis, ranging from -500 to 500.
The left column plots use AUC[0-24h] (ng/ml-h) on the X-axis, ranging from 500 to 2500. The right column plots use C_{max} (ng/ml) on the X-axis, ranging from 100 to 500.
In all four plots, the data points are widely scattered, and the fitted curves (representing the relationship between exposure and the biomarker change) show no clear, consistent trend, indicating no apparent exposure-response relationship for these endpoints.
L2233: Figure showing four scatter plots illustrating the relationship between pharmacokinetic (PK) parameters (AUC[0-24h] and Cmax) and safety endpoints (Maximum change from baseline 6-minute walk distance and Maximum Predicted Forced Vital Capacity, Sitting (%)) for two treatments: ATB200/AT2221 (red dots) and Alglucosidase Alfa/Placebo (blue dots). The plots show a general trend of increasing safety endpoints with increasing PK exposure.
L2234: Figure 5 displays 16 Exposure-Response (E-R) plots for safety endpoints (TEAEs) of Cipaglucosidase Alfa, ATB200-03 and ATB200-02 (Cohorts 1 and 3). The Y-axis represents the Probability of Adverse Event (P(AE)) from 0.00 to 0.75. The X-axis represents ATB200 AUC (ug/ml*hr) from 1000 to 2500. The endpoints analyzed are: Arthralgia, Back pain, Diarrhoea, Fall, Fatigue, Headache (including migraine), Muscle spasms, Musculoskeletal pain, Myalgia, Nasopharyngitis, Nausea, Oropharyngeal pain, Pain in extremity, and Urinary tract infection. Most plots show a positive E-R relationship, with P(AE) increasing as AUC increases. The plot for Nasopharyngitis shows a steep increase in P(AE) with increasing AUC.
L2235: Figure 6 displays 12 exposure-response plots showing the relationship between AT2221 AUC (hr*ng/ml) on the x-axis and P(AE) (Probability of Adverse Event) on the y-axis for various adverse events. The adverse events are: Arthralgia, Back pain, Diarrhoea, Fall, Fatigue, Headache (including migraine), Muscle spasms, Musculoskeletal pain, Myalgia, Nasopharyngitis, Nausea, Oropharyngeal pain, Pain in extremity, and Urinary tract infection. The plots show a generally U-shaped or inverted U-shaped relationship for most events, indicating a potential minimum risk exposure level.

Figure 9 is a forest plot showing the estimated mean treatment difference in Forced Vital Capacity (FVC) across various subgroups. The x-axis ranges from -8 to 17. The overall estimated difference is 2.3 (95% CI: 0.0, 4.6). Subgroups showing positive differences (favoring cipa-mig) include Female (3.8), < 65 years (3.0), White (2.7), Europe (3.4), Experienced ERT status (4.1), ≤ 5 years (4.3), > 5 years (4.2), and ≤ 360 6MWD (4.6). Subgroups showing negative differences (favoring comparator) include Male (1.6), ≥ 65 years (4.7), Non-white (3.6), North/South America (2.2), Asia Pacific (2.6), Naive ERT status (-1.8), > 360 6MWD (0.8), and > 70 baseline FVC (2.2).

L2251: Figure 22 displays four bar charts showing GAA Enzyme Activity (nmol/mg protein/hr) in Quadriceps, Triceps, Gastrocnemius, and Heart tissues after 3 months of treatment. The Y-axis ranges from 0 to 25 nmol/mg protein/hr. The X-axis shows five groups: Vehicle, Alglucosidase alfa, ATB200, ATB200 + AT2221, and WT (Wild Type). In all four tissues, the WT group shows the highest activity (around 20-22 nmol/mg protein/hr). The Vehicle group shows the lowest activity (around 0.7-0.9 nmol/mg protein/hr). The treated groups (Alglucosidase alfa, ATB200, and ATB200 + AT2221) show intermediate activity, generally around 4-6 nmol/mg protein/hr, indicating a significant increase compared to Vehicle but not reaching WT levels.
L2252: Figure 23 displays four bar charts showing GAA Activity (nmol/mg protein/hr) in tissues of Gaa KO mice after 6 months of treatment. The tissues are Quadriceps, Triceps, Gastrocnemius, and Heart. The treatment groups are Vehicle (black), Alglucosidase alfa (green), ATB200 (red), ATB200 + AT2221 (red with pattern), and WT (wild-type, gray). In all tissues, WT activity is significantly higher than all treated groups. In Quadriceps, Gastrocnemius, and Heart, Alglucosidase alfa shows the highest activity among the treated groups, followed by ATB200 + AT2221, ATB200, and Vehicle. L2253: Figure 24 displays two graphs showing muscle function assessments in Gaa KO mice over 6 months. The left graph, 'Wire Hang', plots Latency (s) versus time (Baseline, 1 month, 2 months, 3 months, 4 months, 5 months, 6 months). The right graph, 'Grip Strength', plots Maximum grip (g) versus time. Both graphs compare WT, Vehicle, Alglucosidase alfa, EB ATB200, and EB ATB200 + AT2221 groups. In the Wire Hang assay, WT latency is high and stable (~110s). Vehicle, Alglucosidase alfa, and EB ATB200 groups show a significant decrease in latency over time, with EB ATB200 + AT2221 showing the least decline. In the Grip Strength assay, WT grip strength is high and stable (~150g). Vehicle, Alglucosidase alfa, and EB ATB200 groups show a significant increase in grip strength over time, with EB ATB200 + AT2221 showing the highest grip strength at 6 months.

L2255: Line graph showing Normalized GAA Activity versus Time (Hours) for ATB200 in Human Blood. The Y-axis is Normalized GAA Activity (0 to 120) and the X-axis is Time (Hours) (0 to 4). ATB200 + 17 uM AT2221 (red line) shows moderate stabilization, dropping from 100% to approximately 75% activity by 4 hours. ATB200 + 170 uM AT2221 (blue line) shows the best stabilization, dropping from 100% to approximately 95% activity by 4 hours.

The concentration in both tissues decreases significantly within the first 24 hours, reaching a plateau around 0.1 \mu\text{M} after 96 hours.

L2316: Figure 94: FVC Over Time for Patients With Missing Values at Week 52, Trial ATB200-03. The graph plots Forced Vital Capacity (FVC) in percent predicted versus Study Week (0 to 52). Two treatment groups are compared: Alglucosidase alfa/Placebo (red dashed line) and Cipaglucosidase alfa/Miglustat (blue dashed line). Both groups show a decline in FVC over time. The legend indicates lines for Discontinued and Not discontinued patients. Several patients are highlighted with a gray box labeled (b) (6).
L2317: Figure 95: Empirical Cumulative Distribution Function of 52-Week Change From Baseline in 6MWD. The figure consists of two panels. The left panel, 'Including all patients', shows the cumulative distribution function (CDF) for the Comparator (red line) and Cipa-mig (blue line) groups. The right panel, 'Excluding Subject (b) (6)', shows the CDF for the same groups after removing the outlier. Both panels plot Cumulative Distribution Function (%) versus 52 week change from baseline of 6MWD (meters).
